   > Tip: now every player on the server can have a Seed of their own.

### Creation

*The Seed is planted. Now shape what grows.*

1. **A New World**: checkmark *(after: Seed of a New World)*
   > You took the world apart, element by element, and learned to build it again.
   > Fire, steel, acid, oil, silicon, the atom, antimatter. Every tier was a lesson in chemistry, and you mastered them all.
   > The worlds out there are blank canvases now. Go make them yours.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > What now: this chapter is for after the Seed. Nothing here gates anything; it's what a demigod does with full power.
   > To the right: making matter. Rewrite nuclei with antimatter, compress a singularity, build armour from a collapsed star.
   > To the left: walking the worlds you can now reach, and holding the rarest parts of the periodic table in your hand.
2. **A Sample of Somewhere**: `warbuckscore:biome_capsule` *(after: A New World)*
   > Every world you have walked through, you can carry with you.
   > What: a Biome Capsule records the biome you stand in (sneak + right-click). It tells the Terraformer what to grow.
   > How: glass bottle, rubber sheet and any sapling. Travel to the biome you want, then record it.
   > Tip: capsules stack until filled; each one holds one biome.
3. **The Terraformer**: `warbuckscore:terraformer` *(after: A Sample of Somewhere)*
   > The Seed was never meant to stay in your pocket.
   > What: the Terraformer (Warbucks Core) rewrites the biome around it, nearest chunks first, out to a 9x9 chunk area.
   > How: craft it from the Seed of a New World, processing units, stainless plates and energy cores. Right-click it with a filled Biome Capsule, then with Charged Energy Cores (each powers 9 chunks; sneak to insert a stack). Right-click empty-handed for status.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > What changes: grass and leaf colours, weather (rain or snow), and which mobs spawn, just like vanilla /fillbiome. Blocks you placed stay as they are.
   > Tip: you can change it again later: a new capsule restarts the spread with the new biome.
4. **A World You Chose**: checkmark *(after: The Terraformer)*
   > This is what the power was for.
   > What: let a Terraformer finish its whole area (status shows 81 / 81), then tick this off.
   > Tip: 9 Charged Energy Cores finish one Terraformer.
5. **Nucleosynthesis**: `mekanism:antiprotonic_nucleosynthesizer` *(after: A New World)*
   > You remember making things out of nothing. This is close.
   > What: Mekanism's Antiprotonic Nucleosynthesizer feeds a few millibuckets of antimatter into an item and rebuilds it as something rarer.
   > Chemistry: nucleosynthesis is how stars make elements: lighter nuclei fuse into heavier ones. This machine borrows the idea.
   > How: check JEI for the recipe and the full list of transmutations. Pipe antimatter in from your SPS.
   > Tip: it works slowly. Give it its own antimatter tank.
6. **Hearts of the Sea**: 4x `minecraft:heart_of_the_sea` *(after: Nucleosynthesis)*
   > What: four Hearts of the Sea, each nucleosynthesized from a Nether Star with 5 mB of antimatter.
   > How: Nether Star in, antimatter in, wait about a minute.
   > Tip: a Heart of the Sea plus nautilus shells makes a Conduit: permanent water breathing and night vision around an ocean base.
7. **Skulls on Demand**: 6x `minecraft:wither_skeleton_skull` *(after: Hearts of the Sea)*
   > What: six wither skeleton skulls, made from ordinary skeleton skulls with 5 mB of antimatter each.
   > How: skeleton skulls drop when a charged creeper kills a skeleton, or from other sources (check JEI). Feed them to the Nucleosynthesizer.
   > Tip: that's enough for two more Withers, and two more Nether Stars for a second Seed.
8. **Echoes**: 32x `minecraft:echo_shard` *(after: Skulls on Demand)*
   > What: 32 echo shards, rebuilt from amethyst shards with 2 mB of antimatter each.
   > Chemistry: real amethyst is quartz (SiO₂) coloured purple by traces of iron and natural radiation. Here it becomes something no chemist could make.
   > How: amethyst shards from geodes or a budding amethyst farm. Nucleosynthesizer, 2 mB antimatter each.
   > Tip: echo shards make Recovery Compasses, which point to where you last died.
9. **Sixty-Four Nukes**: 64x `modern_industrialization:nuke` *(after: A New World)*
   > What: MI's Nuke block. Sixty-four of them are crushed together to make a singularity.
   > Chemistry: a nuke combines fission (plutonium) and fusion fuel (deuterium and tritium, the heavy isotopes of hydrogen) around industrial TNT.
   > How: MI Assembler: 6 industrial TNT, 4 plutonium ingots, 2 processing units, 4 nuclear alloy plates, 100 mB deuterium, 50 mB tritium → 2 nukes.
   > Tip: that's 32 crafts, 64 processing units and 128 plutonium ingots. Automate it.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Deep dive: two kinds of nuclear energy
   > Fission splits heavy nuclei like Pu-239 into lighter ones. Fusion joins light nuclei like deuterium (²H) and tritium (³H) into helium.
   > Both release energy because the products are more tightly bound than the inputs. Iron-56 sits at the bottom of that curve: nothing is gained by splitting it or fusing it.
   > Real thermonuclear weapons use a fission stage to compress and heat a fusion stage. MI's recipe follows the same idea.
10. **A Singularity**: `modern_industrialization:singularity` *(after: Sixty-Four Nukes)*
   > What: MI's Singularity: an ultradense metal ball collapsed by 64 nukes in the Implosion Compressor.
   > How: ultradense metal ball (tungsten large plates, lead plates and an iridium plate, check JEI) + 64 nukes → Implosion Compressor.
   > Tip: it's never used up in the Electrolyzer: it slowly bleeds out UU-matter, 1 mB every 2500 ticks. Build several Electrolyzers and several singularities.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Deep dive: collapse
   > A real singularity is the centre of a black hole, where matter is crushed past every limit we know. Stars more than about 20 times the Sun's mass end this way.
   > Before that, there's neutron-star matter: electrons are pushed into protons until only neutrons remain. A teaspoon would weigh about a billion tonnes.
   > MI's singularity is a game item, not physics. But the idea, matter compressed until it becomes a source of something new, fits a demigod well.
11. **Quantum Upgrade**: `modern_industrialization:quantum_upgrade` *(after: A Singularity)*
   > What: the part that turns netherite armour into MI quantum armour.
   > How: MI Assembler: 32 highly advanced upgrades, 8 quantum circuits, 1 singularity, 1 quantum circuit board, 50 mB UU-matter.
   > Tip: 50 mB of UU-matter takes one Electrolyzer and singularity about 1.7 hours (1 mB per 2500 ticks). More singularities, more Electrolyzers.
12. **Quantum Armour**: `modern_industrialization:quantum_helmet`; `modern_industrialization:quantum_chestplate`; `modern_industrialization:quantum_leggings`; `modern_industrialization:quantum_boots` *(after: Quantum Upgrade)*
   > You remember not needing armour. This is the next best thing.
   > What: MI's quantum armour: a full set, each piece a netherite piece plus a quantum upgrade.
   > How: MI Packer: netherite armour piece + 1 quantum upgrade, 200 ticks at 1,000,000 EU/t. Four upgrades for four pieces.
   > Tip: that draw is far above a plain Packer's limit. Check JEI and the MI guidebook for how machine upgrades raise it.
13. **Replicator**: `modern_industrialization:replicator` *(after: Quantum Upgrade)*
   > What: MI's Replicator copies items using UU-matter.
   > How: MI Assembler: quantum machine hull, 2 quantum upgrades, 4 quantum circuits, 500 mB UU-matter. Check JEI for what it can copy.
   > Tip: 500 mB is about 17 hours for one Electrolyzer. A long-term goal for a server, not a weekend.
14. **A Star in a Box**: `modern_industrialization:fusion_reactor`; `modern_industrialization:plasma_turbine` *(after: A New World)*
   > What: MI's fusion reactor makes helium plasma; the plasma turbine turns it into a flood of EU.
   > Chemistry: D + T → ⁴He + n. MI's reactor turns 1000 mB each of deuterium and tritium into helium plasma and hydrogen.
   > How: fusion reactor: 6 fusion chambers, a quantum machine hull and 2 quantum circuits in the MI Assembler. The multiblock layout is in JEI and the MI guidebook.
   > Tip: deuterium-deuterium and helium-3 routes exist too (check JEI under the fusion reactor).
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Deep dive: why fusion is hard
   > Nuclei are positively charged and repel each other. To fuse, they need temperatures over 100 million °C, hotter than the Sun's core.
   > The Sun manages at 15 million °C only because of its crushing gravity and its sheer size. On Earth we use magnetic bottles (tokamaks) or lasers instead.
   > In 2022 the US National Ignition Facility got more energy out of a fusion shot than its lasers put in, for the first time.
15. **Sixty-Four Superconductors**: 64x `modern_industrialization:superconductor_ingot` *(after: A Star in a Box)*
   > What: a stack of MI superconductor ingots, the material of MI's best cables and coils.
   > Chemistry: below a critical temperature, a superconductor carries current with zero resistance. Real high-temperature ones, like YBa₂Cu₃O₇, are copper oxides with yttrium and rare earths.
   > How: Mixer: iridium, annealed copper, yttrium and neodymium dusts → superconductor dust. Electric Blast Furnace → hot ingot → Vacuum Freezer.
   > Tip: 64 ingots need 64 EBF runs at 128 EU/t. Run several furnaces.
16. **Shining Alloy**: 8x `mekanism_extras:alloy_shining` *(after: Sixty-Four Superconductors)*
   > What: Mekanism Extras' alloys go beyond atomic: radiance → thermonuclear → shining → spectrum. Shining is the first that needs antimatter.
   > How: enriched thermonuclear material + antimatter in the Nucleosynthesizer gives enriched shining. Turn it into shining infusion and infuse a thermonuclear alloy (check JEI for each step).
   > Tip: these alloys build Mekanism Extras' higher tiers of factories, cables and energy cubes.
17. **A Stack of Atomic Alloy**: 64x `mekanism:alloy_atomic` *(after: Shining Alloy)*
   > What: 64 atomic alloys, Mekanism's top standard alloy.
   > How: Metallurgic Infuser: reinforced alloy + refined obsidian dust (check JEI). Each step up uses the alloy before it.
   > Tip: a Metallurgic Infusing Factory makes this a matter of minutes.
18. **Quantum Compressor**: `extendedcrafting:compressor` *(after: A New World)*
   > What: Extended Crafting's Quantum Compressor squeezes huge amounts of one material into a singularity.
   > How: crafting table: black iron ingots, a black iron slate, 2 elite components, 2 elite catalysts and a frame (check JEI).
   > Tip: it uses 5,000 FE/t and holds 10 million FE. Power it well.
19. **Your First Singularity**: `extendedcrafting:singularity` *(after: Quantum Compressor)*
   > What: any Extended Crafting singularity: iron, copper, gold, steel, aluminium, platinum and more.
   > How: insert 10,000 of one material into the Quantum Compressor, plus 5 million FE. Check JEI for the full list.
   > Tip: iron or copper is the easiest first pick: your ore lines make thousands an hour.
20. **Ultimate Singularity**: `extendedcrafting:ultimate_singularity` *(after: Your First Singularity)*
   > What: one of every singularity, combined on the Ultimate Table.
   > How: there are 19 singularities in this pack (check JEI for the list). That's about 190,000 items in total.
   > Tip: this is a server project. Split the materials between players.
21. **Worlds Within Worlds**: 64x `compactmachines:wall`; visit `compactmachines:compact_world` *(after: A New World)*
   > What: Compact Machines are small rooms in their own dimension. You've made a new world; now make a few small ones.
   > How: 64 machine walls, then step inside any of your machines with the Personal Shrinking Device.
   > Tip: one room per production line keeps your main base clean and your server's tick time low.
22. **Rare Ground**: find biome `minecraft:mushroom_fields`; find biome `minecraft:ice_spikes`; find biome `minecraft:cherry_grove` *(after: A New World)*
   > You remember these places from above. Walk them this time.
   > What: visit a Mushroom Fields island, an Ice Spikes plain and a Cherry Grove.
   > How: Explorer's Compass or Nature's Compass (if you have one) finds biomes; otherwise fly and look. Each completes on arrival.
   > Tip: Mushroom Fields never spawn hostile mobs. A good place for a quiet base.
23. **Under Everything**: find biome `minecraft:deep_dark`; find biome `deeperdarker:echoing_forest`; find biome `undergarden:frostfields` *(after: Rare Ground)*
   > What: the Deep Dark, the Otherside's Echoing Forest, and the Undergarden's Frostfields.
   > How: the Deep Dark is under mountains in the Overworld; the Echoing Forest is in Deeper and Darker's Otherside; the Frostfields are in the Undergarden.
   > Tip: the Warden still doesn't care how powerful you are. Sneak.
24. **Fire Below**: find biome `minecraft:soul_sand_valley`; find biome `biomesoplenty:crystalline_chasm`; find biome `biomesoplenty:withered_abyss` *(after: Under Everything)*
   > What: a Soul Sand Valley and two of Biomes O' Plenty's rarer Nether biomes: the Crystalline Chasm and the Withered Abyss.
   > Chemistry: soul sand and soul soil burn with a blue flame. Real blue flames mean complete combustion; these are just strange.
   > Tip: the Withered Abyss has bottomless drops. Bring a way to fly.
25. **Twilight Wanderer**: find biome `twilightforest:enchanted_forest`; find biome `twilightforest:glacier`; find biome `twilightforest:highlands` *(after: Fire Below)*
   > What: the Enchanted Forest, the Glacier and the Highlands of the Twilight Forest.
   > How: the Glacier and Highlands are locked behind Twilight bosses. If a biome hurts you, you haven't beaten the boss that guards it yet.
   > Tip: the Enchanted Forest is small and rare. The Magic Map shows biomes as you explore.
26. **Gardens of the End**: find biome `minecraft:end_highlands`; find biome `biomesoplenty:end_wilds`; find biome `biomesoplenty:end_reef`; find biome `biomesoplenty:end_corruption` *(after: Twilight Wanderer)*
   > What: the End Highlands and three Biomes O' Plenty End biomes: the Wilds, the Reef and the Corruption.
   > How: take an End Gateway out past the main island. The BOP biomes are scattered among the outer islands.
   > Tip: End Cities in the Highlands hold Elytra and shulker shells.
27. **Other Skies**: find biome `ad_astra:martian_polar_caps`; find biome `ad_astra:infernal_venus_barrens`; find biome `ad_astra:mercury_deltas`; find biome `ad_astra:glacio_ice_peaks` *(after: Gardens of the End)*
   > What: Mars's polar caps, Venus's infernal barrens, Mercury's deltas and Glacio's ice peaks.
   > Chemistry: Mars's polar caps are water ice under a layer of frozen CO₂ (dry ice). Venus's air is 96% CO₂ at 90 times Earth's pressure.
   > How: Tier 2 rocket for Mars, Tier 3 for Venus and Mercury, Tier 4 for Glacio. Netherite space suit for the hot ones.
   > Tip: set up a launch pad on each planet before you go exploring.
28. **A Breath of Noble Gas**: 64x any of `or(item(chemlib:helium)item(chemlib:neon)item(chemlib:argon)item(chemlib:krypton)item(chemlib:xenon)item(chemlib:radon))` *(after: A New World)*
   > What: any 64 noble gas items from ChemLib, in any mix.
   > Chemistry: noble gases have full outer electron shells, so they almost never react. Neon glows red in a discharge tube; xenon glows blue; radon is radioactive.
   > How: Alchemistry Dissolver on air-rich or gas-bearing items, or the Fusion Chamber (check JEI for which inputs give which gas).
   > Tip: helium is the lightest: fuse two hydrogen and you're there.
29. **The Rare Earths**: 64x any of `or(item(chemlib:lanthanum)item(chemlib:cerium)item(chemlib:praseodymium)item(chemlib:neodymium)item(chemlib:promethium)item(chemlib:samarium)item(chemlib:europium)item(chemlib:gadolinium)item(chemlib:terbium)item(chemlib:dysprosium)item(chemlib:holmium)item(chemlib:erbium)item(chemlib:thulium)item(chemlib:ytterbium)item(chemlib:lutetium))` *(after: A Breath of Noble Gas)*
   > What: any 64 lanthanide items from ChemLib: lanthanum through lutetium.
   > Chemistry: the lanthanides fill the 4f electron shell, which sits deep inside the atom. That's why they're so alike and so hard to separate.
   > How: Fusion Chamber (pairs that add up to 57-71) or the Dissolver on rare-earth-bearing items (check JEI).
   > Tip: neodymium makes the strongest permanent magnets; europium makes red phosphors in screens.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Deep dive: why 'rare' earths?
   > They aren't rare. Cerium is about as common as copper in the Earth's crust. They're 'rare' because they never form rich deposits on their own.
   > Separating them took chemists over a century. Their outer electrons are nearly the same, so their chemistry is nearly the same; only the slow shrinking of the atoms across the row (the lanthanide contraction) tells them apart.
   > Industry separates them by solvent extraction, hundreds of stages long. Your Fusion Chamber skips all that.
30. **Island of Stability**: 64x any of `or(item(chemlib:oganesson)item(chemlib:tennessine)item(chemlib:livermorium)item(chemlib:moscovium)item(chemlib:flerovium)item(chemlib:nihonium))` *(after: The Rare Earths)*
   > What: any 64 of the six newest elements: nihonium, flerovium, moscovium, livermorium, tennessine and oganesson.
   > Chemistry: all six were made in the last 25 years, a few atoms at a time, and decay within seconds or less.
   > How: Alchemistry Fusion Chamber. Automate the inputs and let it run.
   > Tip: physicists predict an 'island of stability' near 114 protons and 184 neutrons, where superheavy nuclei might last much longer. No one has reached it yet.
31. **Demigod**: checkmark *(after: Quantum Armour, A Stack of Atomic Alloy, Your First Singularity, Other Skies, Island of Stability, Echoes)*
   > You don't remember being this any more. You simply are.
   > What: you've walked every world, built armour from a collapsed star, and held the whole periodic table.
   > Now: build. A city, a garden on Mars, a pocket world for every friend on the server. The tools are yours.
   > The new world is waiting. It's whatever you make it.
32. **Pressure Not Required**: 64x `minecraft:diamond` *(after: Nucleosynthesis)*
   > What: the Nucleosynthesizer turns coal into diamond with 4 mB of antimatter. You found it.
   > Chemistry: both are carbon. Graphite and diamond differ only in how the atoms bond: sheets of rings versus a rigid 3D lattice. Nature needs about 50,000 atmospheres to make the change.
   > Tip: 64 diamonds from 64 coal. Your younger self, swinging an iron pickaxe, would be furious.
33. **Where It Began**: find biome `biomesoplenty:origin_valley` *(after: Rare Ground)*
   > What: Biomes O' Plenty's Origin Valley, built to look like the very first versions of Minecraft.
   > Bright grass, roses, plain old trees. You remember a world this simple. It was the first one you made.
   > Tip: it's rare. If you found it by chance, consider it a gift.

## Chemistry Lab

### The Periodic Table

*Rebuild the table, group by group. Each column shares a chemistry, because each shares an outer-electron arrangement.*

1. **Reading the Table**: checkmark *(after: First Entries in the Periodic Table)*
   > You remember this chart. You used to know every box by heart.
   > What: the periodic table orders the elements by proton number. Rows are periods; columns are groups.
   > Chemistry: elements in the same group have the same number of outer (valence) electrons, so they react alike.
   > How: press U on any element in JEI to see which blocks the Dissolver turns into it.
   > Tip: this chapter is a side path. Nothing in the main story waits on it, but it pays well.
2. **Alkali Metals**: 8x `chemlib:lithium`; 8x `chemlib:sodium`; 8x `chemlib:potassium` *(after: Reading the Table)*
   > What: lithium, sodium and potassium, the softest and most reactive metals.
   > Chemistry: one valence electron, easily lost. Sodium fizzes on water (2Na + 2H₂O → 2NaOH + H₂); potassium burns lilac.
   > How: end stone gives lithium; lapis lazuli gives sodium (the mineral lazurite is a sodium aluminosilicate). Plants give potassium.
   > Tip: check JEI with U on each element. Many common blocks hide small amounts.
3. **Alkaline Earth Metals**: 4x `chemlib:beryllium`; 8x `chemlib:magnesium`; 8x `chemlib:calcium` *(after: Alkali Metals)*
   > What: beryllium, magnesium and calcium: lighter structural metals and the stuff of bones and limestone.
   > Chemistry: two valence electrons, so they form 2+ ions. Calcium carbonate (CaCO₃) is limestone; magnesium burns blinding white.
   > How: andesite and bone give calcium (bone via hydroxylapatite, the mineral in teeth). Beryllium and magnesium come out of their compounds: check JEI.
4. **Transition Metals**: 16x `chemlib:iron`; 16x `chemlib:copper`; 8x `chemlib:nickel`; 8x `chemlib:zinc`; 4x `chemlib:gold` *(after: Alkaline Earth Metals)*
   > What: the hard, conductive metals behind every machine you've built.
   > Chemistry: their d-orbitals fill across the row. That's why they form coloured compounds and make good catalysts.
   > How: dissolve ingots, nuggets or dusts of each metal.
   > Tip: ore you already process gives nuggets and dust. Dissolve the spares, not your ingots.
5. **Post-Transition Metals**: 8x `chemlib:aluminum`; 8x `chemlib:tin`; 8x `chemlib:lead`; 4x `chemlib:indium` *(after: Transition Metals)*
   > What: aluminum, tin, lead and indium: softer, lower-melting metals just right of the transition block.
   > Chemistry: indium tin oxide is the clear conductor in every touchscreen. Lead is dense enough to stop X-rays.
   > How: diorite dissolves into tin and indium. Aluminum and lead come from their ingots, dusts and minerals (check JEI).
   > Perk: hold one of each of these four elements and your body remembers them: +2 armor toughness, permanently. Soft metals bend instead of breaking.
   > Tip: type /warbucks elements to see which Periodic Table perks you've unlocked and what's still missing.
6. **Liquid Metals**: 4x `chemlib:mercury`; 4x `chemlib:gallium` *(after: Transition Metals)*
   > What: mercury and gallium, the two metals you can pour (almost) at room temperature.
   > Chemistry: mercury melts at −38.8 °C; gallium at 29.8 °C, below body temperature.
   > How: ender pearls and end stone dissolve into mercury. Cobblestone gives a little gallium.
7. **Challenge: The First Twenty**: `chemlib:hydrogen`; `chemlib:helium`; `chemlib:lithium`; `chemlib:beryllium`; `chemlib:boron`; `chemlib:carbon`; `chemlib:nitrogen`; `chemlib:oxygen`; `chemlib:fluorine`; `chemlib:neon`; `chemlib:sodium`; `chemlib:magnesium`; `chemlib:aluminum`; `chemlib:silicon`; `chemlib:phosphorus`; `chemlib:sulfur`; `chemlib:chlorine`; `chemlib:argon`; `chemlib:potassium`; `chemlib:calcium` *(after: Noble Gases)*
   > What: one of each of elements 1 to 20, the four shortest rows of the table.
   > Chemistry: these twenty make up almost all of your body, the oceans and the air.
   > How: most come from the groups in this chapter. Boron and the noble gases need the Fusion Chamber (boron is 5: try H + Be).
   > Tip: an optional collection. Keep one of each in a chest as you go.
8. **The Coinage Metals**: 8x `chemlib:copper`; 8x `chemlib:silver`; 8x `chemlib:gold` *(after: Transition Metals)*
   > What: the three metals humans have minted into money for thousands of years.
   > Chemistry: they're unreactive enough to keep their shine. Silver is the best electrical conductor of any element.
   > How: deepslate dissolves into a little silver. Check JEI for richer sources.
9. **Metalloids**: 4x `chemlib:germanium`; 16x `chemlib:silicon`; 4x `chemlib:arsenic` *(after: Reading the Table)*
   > What: germanium, silicon and arsenic sit on the staircase between metals and non-metals.
   > Chemistry: silicon conducts a little, and a trace of arsenic (doping) controls how much. The first transistor (1947) was germanium.
   > How: sand dissolves into silicon dioxide, which dissolves again into silicon and oxygen. Blaze powder and Nether bricks give germanium.
   > Tip: you'll need a lot of silicon later, for circuits and AE2.
10. **Non-Metals**: 16x `chemlib:nitrogen`; 8x `chemlib:phosphorus`; 16x `chemlib:sulfur` *(after: Metalloids)*
   > What: nitrogen, phosphorus and sulfur. Life and industry both run on them.
   > Chemistry: nitrogen and phosphorus feed every crop; sulfur becomes sulfuric acid, the most-made chemical on Earth.
   > How: check JEI with U. Nether blocks are a good place to look for sulfur.
11. **Chalcogens**: 16x `chemlib:oxygen`; 16x `chemlib:sulfur`; 4x `chemlib:selenium` *(after: Non-Metals)*
   > What: oxygen, sulfur and selenium. 'Chalcogen' means 'ore-former'.
   > Chemistry: two electrons short of a full shell, so they grab metals: most ores are oxides or sulfides (Fe₂O₃, ZnS, PbS).
   > How: fish and crimson fungus dissolve into selenium. Oxygen comes from nearly everything.
   > Tip: selenium is a trace nutrient: you need a little, and too much is poisonous.
12. **Halogens**: 4x `chemlib:fluorine`; 8x `chemlib:chlorine`; 4x `chemlib:bromine`; 4x `chemlib:iodine` *(after: Chalcogens)*
   > What: fluorine, chlorine, bromine and iodine, the most reactive non-metals.
   > Chemistry: one electron short of full. 'Halogen' means 'salt-maker': Na + Cl → NaCl, table salt.
   > How: magma blocks give fluorine and bromine. Horn coral gives iodine (via lead iodide). Chlorine comes out of salts: check JEI.
13. **Noble Gases**: 8x `chemlib:helium`; 8x `chemlib:neon`; 8x `chemlib:argon` *(after: Halogens, Post-Transition Metals, EV: Processing Units)*
   > Two halves of the table, joined. More of the old knowledge settles into place.
   > What: helium, neon and argon, the gases that refuse to react.
   > Chemistry: a full outer shell means nothing to gain or lose. Argon shields welds from air; neon glows red in a discharge tube.
   > How: nothing dissolves into them, so build them: the Alchemistry Fusion Chamber (EV) adds proton numbers, so H + H → He. Check JEI for neon and argon.
   > Tip: real stars fuse hydrogen into helium too, though it takes them four protons, not two.
14. **Heavy Noble Gases**: 2x `chemlib:xenon`; 2x `chemlib:radon` *(after: Post-Transition Metals)*
   > What: xenon and radon, the heaviest natural noble gases.
   > Chemistry: in 1962 xenon was made to react (with platinum hexafluoride), ending the idea that noble gases never react. Radon is radioactive.
   > How: dragon's breath dissolves into both. That means a trip to the End.
15. **Rare Earths**: 4x `chemlib:lanthanum`; 4x `chemlib:cerium`; 4x `chemlib:neodymium` *(after: Noble Gases, EV: Processing Units)*
   > What: the lanthanides: lanthanum, cerium, neodymium and their near-identical neighbours.
   > Chemistry: they differ only in an inner (4f) shell, so their chemistry is almost the same. Neodymium makes the strongest permanent magnets.
   > How: check JEI for sources. The Alchemistry Fusion Chamber can also build them from lighter elements.
16. **Actinides**: 4x `chemlib:thorium`; 8x `chemlib:uranium`; 4x `chemlib:plutonium` *(after: Noble Gases, Transmutation by Neutron)*
   > What: thorium, uranium and plutonium, the fuels of the nuclear age.
   > Chemistry: every actinide is radioactive. Plutonium barely exists in nature: you made it by firing neutrons at uranium.
   > How: dissolve uranium and thorium materials. Plutonium comes from your fission reactor's waste.
17. **Isotopes: Heavy Water**: `mekanism:electric_pump`; `mekanism:upgrade_filter`; `mekanism:heavy_water_bucket` *(after: Alkali Metals, Mekanism: Metallurgic Infuser)*
   > What: heavy water, D₂O: water made with deuterium, hydrogen with an extra neutron.
   > Chemistry: isotopes have the same protons (so the same chemistry) but different neutrons. About 1 in 6,400 hydrogen atoms is deuterium.
   > How: put a Filter Upgrade in a Mekanism Electric Pump and pump from water. It pulls out heavy water instead.
   > Tip: deuterium is half of fusion fuel. You'll want this again in the Power chapter.
18. **Superheavy Elements**: `chemlib:tennessine` *(after: Rare Earths, Actinides)*
   > What: tennessine, element 117. Nothing in the world dissolves into it; you have to build it.
   > Chemistry: real labs make superheavy elements by smashing nuclei together; only a handful of atoms have ever existed.
   > How: the Alchemistry Fusion Chamber joins two elements, adding their proton numbers. Check JEI for the pairs that reach 117.
   > Tip: one more step after this: add hydrogen (1) to tennessine (117) and you get 118.
19. **Element 118**: `chemlib:oganesson` *(after: Superheavy Elements, The Complete Periodic Table)*
   > The last box on the table. You remember now: you once named these.
   > What: oganesson, the heaviest element ever made.
   > Chemistry: in reality it lasts under a millisecond. Here it sits in your inventory, which is the kind of thing demigods can do.
   > How: fuse tennessine with hydrogen (or any pair adding to 118) in the Fusion Chamber.
20. **Covalent Bonds: CO₂**: 4x `chemlib:carbon_dioxide` *(after: Non-Metals)*
   > What: carbon dioxide, your first compound in this chapter.
   > Chemistry: C + O₂ → CO₂. Non-metals don't hand electrons over; they share them in covalent bonds (O=C=O).
   > How: in the Combiner, pick Carbon Dioxide and supply 1 carbon + 2 oxygen.
   > Tip: the Combiner GUI shows each recipe once you select its output.
21. **Hydrocarbons**: 4x `chemlib:methane`; 4x `chemlib:ethylene` *(after: Covalent Bonds: CO₂)*
   > What: methane (natural gas) and ethylene (the building block of polyethylene).
   > Chemistry: CH₄ has four single bonds. C₂H₄ has a C=C double bond, which is what lets it link into plastic chains.
   > How: Combiner: methane = 1 C + 4 H; ethylene = 2 C + 4 H.
22. **Ammonia**: 4x `chemlib:ammonia` *(after: Hydrocarbons)*
   > What: ammonia, NH₃, the base of nearly all fertiliser.
   > Chemistry: N₂ + 3H₂ → 2NH₃. Breaking nitrogen's triple bond is hard; the Haber-Bosch process needs heat, pressure and an iron catalyst.
   > How: Combiner: 1 nitrogen + 3 hydrogen. Later you'll run real Haber-Bosch in an MI machine.
23. **Ionic Bonds: Salt**: 8x `chemlib:sodium_chloride` *(after: Ammonia, Halogens)*
   > What: sodium chloride, NaCl: table salt.
   > Chemistry: sodium gives its one electron to chlorine. Na⁺ and Cl⁻ then hold together in a cubic crystal: an ionic bond.
   > How: Combiner: 1 sodium + 1 chlorine.
   > Tip: a reactive metal plus a poisonous gas makes something you sprinkle on food. Chemistry is strange.
24. **Strong Acids**: 4x `chemlib:hydrochloric_acid`; 4x `chemlib:nitric_acid` *(after: Ionic Bonds: Salt)*
   > What: hydrochloric acid (HCl) and nitric acid (HNO₃).
   > Chemistry: acids give up H⁺ ions in water. Mixed 3:1, HCl and HNO₃ make aqua regia, which dissolves gold and platinum.
   > How: Combiner: HCl = 1 H + 1 Cl; HNO₃ = 1 H + 1 N + 3 O.
25. **King of Chemicals**: 8x `chemlib:sulfuric_acid` *(after: Strong Acids)*
   > Built atom by atom. The old craft is coming back faster now.
   > What: sulfuric acid, H₂SO₄, built straight from elements.
   > Chemistry: industry makes it by the contact process: S → SO₂ → SO₃ → H₂SO₄. Here you skip the plant and place the atoms yourself.
   > How: Combiner: 2 hydrogen + 1 sulfur + 4 oxygen.
26. **Sugar and Wood**: 2x `chemlib:sucrose`; 2x `chemlib:cellulose` *(after: Covalent Bonds: CO₂)*
   > What: sucrose (table sugar) and cellulose (what wood and paper are made of).
   > Chemistry: plants make both from CO₂ and water using sunlight. Cellulose is a chain of glucose rings: C₆H₁₀O₅ per unit.
   > How: Combiner: sucrose = 12 C + 22 H + 11 O; cellulose = 6 C + 10 H + 5 O.
   > Tip: that's a lot of atoms. Stock up on carbon, hydrogen and oxygen first.
27. **Alcohol**: 4x `chemlib:hydroxide`; 2x `chemlib:ethanol` *(after: Hydrocarbons)*
   > What: ethanol, C₂H₅OH, made by attaching a hydroxide (OH) group to a two-carbon chain.
   > Chemistry: the -OH group is what makes it an alcohol and lets it mix with water.
   > How: Combiner: hydroxide = 1 O + 1 H, then ethanol = 2 C + 5 H + 1 hydroxide.
28. **Lye**: 4x `chemlib:sodium_hydroxide` *(after: Ionic Bonds: Salt)*
   > What: sodium hydroxide, NaOH: lye, a strong base.
   > Chemistry: industry makes it by electrolysing salt water: 2NaCl + 2H₂O → 2NaOH + Cl₂ + H₂. Acid + base → salt + water.
   > How: Combiner: 1 sodium + 1 hydroxide (make hydroxide from 1 O + 1 H).
29. **Rust**: 4x `chemlib:iron_oxide` *(after: Strong Acids)*
   > What: iron(III) oxide, Fe₂O₃: rust, and also hematite, the most common iron ore.
   > Chemistry: 4Fe + 3O₂ → 2Fe₂O₃. A blast furnace runs this backwards, using carbon to pull the oxygen off.
   > How: Combiner: 2 iron + 3 oxygen.
30. **Group 1, Complete**: `chemlib:lithium`; `chemlib:sodium`; `chemlib:potassium`; `chemlib:rubidium`; `chemlib:cesium`; `chemlib:francium` *(after: Alkali Metals, Noble Gases)*
   > What: all six alkali metals: Li, Na, K, Rb, Cs and Fr.
   > Chemistry: reactivity climbs down the group: the outer electron sits further from the nucleus and is lost more easily. Caesium explodes in water.
   > How: press U on rubidium and caesium in JEI for Dissolver sources. Francium is rare in both reality and here; the Fusion Chamber can build it.
   > Tip: real francium has a half-life of about 22 minutes. Yours keeps.
31. **Group 2, Complete**: `chemlib:beryllium`; `chemlib:magnesium`; `chemlib:calcium`; `chemlib:strontium`; `chemlib:barium`; `chemlib:radium` *(after: Alkaline Earth Metals, Noble Gases)*
   > What: all six alkaline earth metals: Be, Mg, Ca, Sr, Ba and Ra.
   > Chemistry: strontium salts burn red and barium salts green: that's how fireworks get their colours. Radium glows from its own radioactivity.
   > How: JEI (U) shows Dissolver sources for strontium, barium and radium; the Fusion Chamber fills any gaps.
32. **The Boron Group**: `chemlib:boron`; `chemlib:aluminum`; `chemlib:gallium`; `chemlib:indium`; `chemlib:thallium` *(after: Post-Transition Metals, Noble Gases)*
   > What: boron, aluminum, gallium, indium and thallium.
   > Chemistry: boron is a metalloid; the rest are metals. Borosilicate glass (with B₂O₃) survives sudden heat, which is why lab glassware uses it.
   > How: boron needs the Fusion Chamber (5 = 1 + 4: hydrogen + beryllium). Check JEI for thallium.
   > Tip: thallium is very toxic in reality. In a chest it's harmless.
33. **The Carbon Group**: `chemlib:carbon`; `chemlib:silicon`; `chemlib:germanium`; `chemlib:tin`; `chemlib:lead` *(after: Metalloids, Post-Transition Metals)*
   > What: carbon, silicon, germanium, tin and lead, all with four valence electrons.
   > Chemistry: the group runs from a non-metal (carbon) through metalloids (Si, Ge) to metals (Sn, Pb). Same outer shell, very different behaviour.
   > How: all five come from the Dissolver: coal or graphite, sand, Nether bricks, diorite and lead ingots (check JEI).
34. **The Pnictogens**: `chemlib:nitrogen`; `chemlib:phosphorus`; `chemlib:arsenic`; `chemlib:antimony`; `chemlib:bismuth` *(after: Non-Metals, Metalloids)*
   > What: nitrogen, phosphorus, arsenic, antimony and bismuth.
   > Chemistry: five valence electrons, so they often form three bonds (NH₃, PH₃). Bismuth crystals grow in stepped 'hopper' shapes with a rainbow oxide film.
   > How: JEI (U) shows Dissolver sources for antimony and bismuth.
35. **Group 17, Complete**: `chemlib:fluorine`; `chemlib:chlorine`; `chemlib:bromine`; `chemlib:iodine`; `chemlib:astatine` *(after: Halogens, Noble Gases)*
   > What: the four common halogens plus astatine, one of the rarest natural elements on Earth.
   > Chemistry: down the group the halogens go from gas (F₂, Cl₂) to liquid (Br₂) to solid (I₂): bigger molecules attract each other more strongly.
   > How: astatine has a few Dissolver sources (press U in JEI), or build it in the Fusion Chamber.
36. **Group 18, Complete**: `chemlib:helium`; `chemlib:neon`; `chemlib:argon`; `chemlib:krypton`; `chemlib:xenon`; `chemlib:radon` *(after: Noble Gases, Heavy Noble Gases)*
   > What: helium through radon, the whole right-hand column.
   > Chemistry: from 1960 to 1983 the metre was defined by the orange light of krypton-86. Xenon lights car headlamps and drives ion engines in spacecraft.
   > How: nothing dissolves into the light ones: fuse them in the Fusion Chamber (krypton is 36: try neon + iron). Xenon and radon come from dragon's breath.
37. **All Fifteen Lanthanides**: `chemlib:lanthanum`; `chemlib:cerium`; `chemlib:praseodymium`; `chemlib:neodymium`; `chemlib:promethium`; `chemlib:samarium`; `chemlib:europium`; `chemlib:gadolinium`; `chemlib:terbium`; `chemlib:dysprosium`; `chemlib:holmium`; `chemlib:erbium`; `chemlib:thulium`; `chemlib:ytterbium`; `chemlib:lutetium` *(after: Rare Earths)*
   > What: the full lanthanide row, the first line of the f-block.
   > Chemistry: europium makes the red in old TV screens and the anti-forgery glow in euro notes. Promethium has no stable isotope at all.
   > How: a few have Dissolver sources (press U); the Fusion Chamber builds the rest by adding proton numbers.
   > Tip: a challenge, not a requirement. Work through them as spare elements pile up.
38. **All Fifteen Actinides**: `chemlib:actinium`; `chemlib:thorium`; `chemlib:protactinium`; `chemlib:uranium`; `chemlib:neptunium`; `chemlib:plutonium`; `chemlib:americium`; `chemlib:curium`; `chemlib:berkelium`; `chemlib:californium`; `chemlib:einsteinium`; `chemlib:fermium`; `chemlib:mendelevium`; `chemlib:nobelium`; `chemlib:lawrencium` *(after: Actinides)*
   > What: the full actinide row. Everything past uranium is man-made.
   > Chemistry: einsteinium and fermium were first found in the debris of the first hydrogen bomb test in 1952. Americium sits in household smoke detectors.
   > How: the Fusion Chamber: gold (79) plus a light element reaches actinium and beyond (check JEI for the pairs).
39. **Limestone**: 4x `chemlib:calcium_carbonate` *(after: Covalent Bonds: CO₂, Alkaline Earth Metals)*
   > What: calcium carbonate, the mineral in limestone, chalk, marble and seashells.
   > Chemistry: heat it and it splits: CaCO₃ → CaO + CO₂. That quicklime is the start of cement and mortar.
   > How: Combiner: first carbonate (1 carbon + 3 oxygen), then 1 calcium + 1 carbonate.
40. **Saltpeter**: 4x `chemlib:potassium_nitrate` *(after: Ammonia, Alkali Metals)*
   > What: potassium nitrate, the oxidiser in gunpowder and a classic fertiliser.
   > Chemistry: black powder is roughly 75% KNO₃, 15% charcoal and 10% sulfur. The nitrate brings its own oxygen, so it burns without air.
   > How: Combiner: nitrate (1 nitrogen + 3 oxygen), then 1 potassium + 1 nitrate.
   > Tip: the Combiner also turns potassium nitrate, sulfur and graphite into gunpowder (check JEI).
41. **Aspirin**: 2x `chemlib:acetylsalicylic_acid` *(after: Alcohol)*
   > What: acetylsalicylic acid, the world's best-known painkiller.
   > Chemistry: willow bark contains salicin, used against pain for thousands of years. Adding an acetyl group (1897) made it gentler on the stomach.
   > How: Combiner: 9 carbon + 8 hydrogen + 4 oxygen.
42. **Permanganate**: 2x `chemlib:potassium_permanganate` *(after: Lye)*
   > What: potassium permanganate, deep purple crystals and a strong oxidiser.
   > Chemistry: manganese here is in its +7 oxidation state and eager to take electrons back. The purple fades as it reacts, so chemists use it as its own indicator.
   > How: Combiner: 1 potassium + 1 manganese + 4 oxygen.
43. **Morning Coffee**: `chemlib:caffeine` *(after: Ammonia)*
   > Even a demigod likes a cup in the morning. You remember that much without trying.
   > What: caffeine, built atom by atom: 8 carbon, 10 hydrogen, 4 nitrogen, 2 oxygen.
   > Chemistry: it blocks adenosine receptors in the brain, the signal that tells you you're tired.
   > Tip: plants make caffeine as an insecticide. You drink it on purpose.
44. **Han Purple**: `chemlib:han_purple` *(after: Ionic Bonds: Salt)*
   > What: han purple, BaCuSi₂O₆, a barium copper silicate.
   > Chemistry: Chinese artisans made it more than 2,000 years ago by firing barium, copper and silica minerals together. It colours figures in the Terracotta Army.
   > How: Combiner: 1 barium + 1 copper + 2 silicon + 6 oxygen.
   > Tip: one of the first synthetic pigments in history. You just repeated an ancient experiment.

### Lab Notebook

*Why it works: the real chemistry behind every machine in the pack.*

1. **Note: Why Alloys Are Harder**: checkmark *(after: The First Alloy)*
   > You open a notebook in your own handwriting. The first page is about bronze.
   > What: a pure metal is soft because its atoms sit in neat layers that slide past each other.
   > Chemistry: add a few bigger or smaller atoms (tin in copper) and the layers jam. Bronze is about 88% Cu, 12% Sn.
   > Tip: tick this when you've read both pages. Lab notes never block the main path.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Deep dive: metals are a lattice of positive ions in a 'sea' of shared electrons (metallic bonding). The electrons hold the ions together but let whole planes of atoms shift: that's why metals bend instead of shattering.
   > Solid solutions: in bronze, tin atoms replace some copper atoms in the lattice. Tin atoms are larger, so they distort the planes around them and pin them in place. Harder, stronger, still workable.
   > In the pack: 3 copper dust + 1 tin dust → 3 bronze dust (a 3:1 ratio by count, close to the historical recipe). Every later alloy follows the same idea with different partners.
2. **Note: Steel and Carbon**: checkmark *(after: Steel)*
   > What: steel is iron with up to about 2% carbon. Most structural steel has well under 1%.
   > Chemistry: tiny carbon atoms fit into the gaps between iron atoms (an interstitial alloy) and lock the layers.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > From ore to iron: a real blast furnace burns coke to carbon monoxide, which strips oxygen from iron ore: Fe₂O₃ + 3CO → 2Fe + 3CO₂.
   > Too much carbon: furnace iron ('pig iron') has about 4% carbon and is brittle. Steelmaking blows oxygen through it to burn the excess off as CO and CO₂ (the basic oxygen process).
   > In the pack: MI skips the ore step: iron dust + coke dust → uncooked steel, baked in the Steam Blast Furnace. The coke you made is the carbon source.
3. **Note: Invar, the Metal That Won't Grow**: checkmark *(after: Electric Mixer)*
   > What: invar is about 64% iron and 36% nickel. MI uses it for rotary blades and gears.
   > Chemistry: most metals expand when heated. Invar barely does, which keeps precision parts precise.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Why: in this exact mix, a magnetic effect inside the crystal shrinks the lattice as it warms, cancelling the normal expansion (the 'Invar effect').
   > History: Charles-Édouard Guillaume found it in 1896 and won the 1920 Nobel Prize in Physics. It went into clocks, measuring tapes and later the shadow masks of colour TVs.
   > In the pack: mix iron and nickel dust in the Mixer (check JEI for the ratio). Cupronickel (copper + nickel) is its cousin, used for heating coils because it resists current steadily.
4. **Note: Electrum, a Natural Alloy**: checkmark *(after: Electrum)*
   > What: electrum is gold alloyed with silver. It's found that way in rivers, already mixed.
   > Chemistry: gold and silver atoms are almost the same size and both form the same crystal, so they mix in any proportion.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Complete solubility: when two metals have similar atomic radius, the same crystal structure and similar electronegativity, they can blend smoothly from 0% to 100% (the Hume-Rothery rules). Gold-silver and copper-nickel are classic examples.
   > Why MI wants it: both metals conduct electricity superbly and resist corrosion, ideal for circuit boards.
   > In the pack: gold ore often gives silver as a byproduct in the Centrifuge for exactly this reason: native gold is usually electrum.
5. **Note: Stainless Steel**: checkmark *(after: Stainless Steel)*
   > What: steel with at least 10.5% chromium (plus nickel and manganese) that doesn't rust.
   > Chemistry: chromium reacts with air to form a thin Cr₂O₃ film only a few atoms thick. It seals the surface.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Passivation: ordinary rust (hydrated iron oxide) is flaky, so it falls off and exposes fresh iron: 4Fe + 3O₂ + 2xH₂O → 2Fe₂O₃·xH₂O. Chromium oxide is dense and sticks, so oxygen can't get through.
   > Self-repair: scratch the surface and the exposed chromium reacts with oxygen again within moments, rebuilding the film.
   > In the pack: 6 iron + 1 chromium + 1 nickel + 1 manganese dust → 9 stainless dust. It melts so hot that it leaves the EBF as a hot ingot and must be cooled in the Vacuum Freezer.
6. **Note: Coking Coal**: checkmark *(after: Coke)*
   > What: coke is coal heated in an oven with no air, leaving almost pure carbon.
   > Chemistry: without oxygen the coal can't burn. Instead its volatile parts (tars, gases, water) boil away. This is pyrolysis.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > What leaves: coal tar and creosote (heavy aromatic oils), coal gas (mostly H₂ and CH₄), ammonia and sulfur compounds. Old gasworks sold all of them.
   > What stays: a hard, porous carbon skeleton. It's strong enough to carry the weight of ore in a blast furnace and burns hotter than coal.
   > In the pack: the Coke Oven turns coal into coke and sometimes creosote. Charcoal from wood is the same idea on a smaller scale.
7. **Note: Taking Things Apart**: checkmark *(after: The Dissolver)*
   > What: the Dissolver turns any block into the elements and compounds it's made of.
   > Chemistry: every substance is built from about 90 natural elements. Analysing what's inside is called elemental analysis.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > The real thing: chemists can't dissolve a block of wood into a pile of carbon, but they can measure it. Burn a sample in oxygen and weigh the CO₂ and H₂O formed: that gives the carbon and hydrogen content (combustion analysis). A mass spectrometer reads atoms by their mass.
   > Conservation of mass: atoms are never created or destroyed in a chemical change, only rearranged (Lavoisier, 1789). The Dissolver is a game version of that law: everything you put in comes out as atoms.
   > In the pack: many outputs are chance-based (JEI shows the odds). Some items give compounds first; dissolve those again for pure elements.
8. **Note: Fixed Recipes**: checkmark *(after: The Combiner)*
   > What: the Combiner always needs the same numbers of each element for a compound.
   > Chemistry: that's the law of definite proportions: a pure compound always has the same elements in the same ratio.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Proust's law (1794): water from a river, a glacier or a test tube is always 2 H for every 1 O, which is 11.2% hydrogen by mass. Compounds aren't mixtures; they have a formula.
   > Balanced equations: because atoms are conserved, both sides of a reaction must hold the same atoms: 2H₂ + O₂ → 2H₂O has 4 H and 2 O on each side. The Combiner's recipes are balanced equations made of items.
   > In the pack: compare a Combiner recipe in JEI with the formula on the compound's tooltip; the counts match.
9. **Note: Isotopes**: checkmark *(after: Isotope Separation)*
   > What: isotopes are atoms of one element with different numbers of neutrons.
   > Chemistry: chemistry is decided by electrons, and isotopes have the same electrons, so they react identically. Only their mass differs.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Hydrogen's three: ¹H (no neutron), deuterium ²H (one) and tritium ³H (two). Deuterium is stable and found in heavy water, D₂O; tritium is radioactive and is bred from lithium. Both feed the fusion reactor.
   > Uranium's two: natural uranium is 99.3% U-238 and only 0.72% U-235, the isotope that splits easily. Reactors need it enriched to roughly 3 to 5%.
   > How enrichment works: turn uranium into UF₆, a gas, then spin it. Molecules with U-238 are about 1% heavier and drift outward. Thousands of centrifuges in a row (a cascade) multiply that tiny difference. The Isotopic Centrifuge is one stage of that.
10. **Note: Making Elements**: checkmark *(after: Element Transmutation)*
   > What: Alchemistry's fission and fusion chambers split or join nuclei to change one element into another.
   > Chemistry: an element is defined by its proton count (atomic number). Change the nucleus and you change the element. That's nuclear physics, not chemistry.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > In stars: stars fuse hydrogen into helium, then helium into carbon and oxygen, and so on up to iron. Fusing past iron costs energy instead of releasing it.
   > Heavier than iron: most heavy elements form when nuclei capture neutrons, slowly in old giant stars (the s-process) or in a flash during neutron-star mergers and supernovae (the r-process). Your gold was forged in a cosmic collision.
   > Oganesson (118): made in 2002 by firing calcium-48 (Z = 20) at californium-249 (Z = 98): 20 + 98 = 118. Only a handful of atoms have ever existed, each lasting under a millisecond. The chambers add atomic numbers the same way.
11. **Fool's Gold**: `chemlib:iron_disulfide` *(after: The Combiner)*
   > You remember laughing at prospectors. Then you remember being one.
   > What: you synthesised iron disulfide, FeS₂: pyrite, better known as fool's gold.
   > Chemistry: brassy and cubic, it fooled miners for centuries. Strike it on steel and it sparks; the name comes from the Greek for fire.
   > Tip: roasting pyrite was once a major source of SO₂ for sulfuric acid: 4FeS₂ + 11O₂ → 2Fe₂O₃ + 8SO₂.
12. **Note: Why Crushing Doubles Ore**: checkmark *(after: Steam Macerator)*
   > What: in this pack, every route caps at 2x per ore block (1.5x per raw ore) until you have acids.
   > Chemistry: real ore is a little metal mineral locked in a lot of worthless rock (gangue). Grinding frees the mineral so it can be separated.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Beneficiation: mines crush ore to powder, then concentrate it. Dense minerals sink in water (gravity separation), magnetic ones stick to magnets, and sulfide minerals cling to air bubbles in froth flotation while the rock sinks.
   > Smelting the concentrate: only then is the metal freed by heat and chemistry, for example 2PbS + 3O₂ → 2PbO + 2SO₂, then PbO + C → Pb + CO.
   > The game rule: crushing recovers more of the metal than tossing raw chunks in a furnace, so dust gives more ingots. The 2x cap is a balance choice; the next notes explain how acids push past it.
13. **Note: 3x, 4x, 5x**: checkmark *(after: 5x Ore: Dissolution)*
   > What: Mekanism's ore chain adds a chemical step per tier: O₂ (3x), HCl (4x), H₂SO₄ (5x).
   > Chemistry: each step mirrors a real way of pulling metal out of rock: roasting, chlorination and leaching.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > 3x, oxygen: roasting sulfide ore in air turns it into an oxide that is easier to reduce: 2ZnS + 3O₂ → 2ZnO + 2SO₂.
   > 4x, chloride: metal chlorides are often volatile or soluble, so chlorination separates metals cleanly. Titanium is refined this way (the Kroll process goes through TiCl₄).
   > 5x, leaching: sulfuric acid dissolves the metal out as a sulfate slurry, e.g. CuO + H₂SO₄ → CuSO₄ + H₂O. Wash it, crystallise it, and almost nothing is left behind. This hydrometallurgy is how much of the world's copper is made today.
14. **Note: Ores Come in Families**: checkmark *(after: Ore Byproducts)*
   > What: centrifuging Mekanism dirty dust gives the main metal plus a chance at a second one.
   > Chemistry: metals with similar chemistry crystallise together in the same rocks, so real refineries recover them as byproducts.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > The pairs in this pack: iron → nickel (iron-nickel ores), copper → gold (recovered when copper is refined), gold → silver (native gold is electrum), lead → silver (galena, PbS, is the main silver source), tin → tungsten (cassiterite sits with wolframite), osmium → iridium (osmiridium), uranium → lead (uranium decays into lead over billions of years).
   > Why it matters: many rare metals have no mines of their own. Most of the world's indium, gallium and germanium comes out of zinc and aluminum refining.
15. **Note: Refining Platinum**: checkmark *(after: The Platinum Line)*
   > What: the platinum line turns anode sludge into pure platinum and four rarer metals.
   > Chemistry: platinum resists almost every acid, but aqua regia (3 HCl : 1 HNO₃) dissolves it. Then a salt pulls it back out alone.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Step 1, dissolve: nitric acid oxidises the metal and chloride holds it in solution: Pt + 4HNO₃ + 6HCl → H₂PtCl₆ + 4NO₂ + 4H₂O.
   > Step 2, precipitate: ammonium chloride forms a yellow salt that barely dissolves: H₂PtCl₆ + 2NH₄Cl → (NH₄)₂PtCl₆ + 2HCl.
   > Step 3, calcine: heat drives everything else off as gas: 3(NH₄)₂PtCl₆ → 3Pt + 2NH₄Cl + 16HCl + 2N₂.
   > The residue: iridium, osmium, rhodium and ruthenium don't dissolve in aqua regia, so they stay behind and are separated later (the Centrifuge in this pack).
16. **Coffee Break**: `chemlib:caffeine` *(after: The Combiner)*
   > Even a demigod needs a coffee before the long reactions.
   > What: you synthesised caffeine, C₈H₁₀N₄O₂, from its elements.
   > Chemistry: caffeine fits the brain's adenosine receptors without switching them on, so the 'you're tired' signal can't get through.
   > Tip: it's a purine, a close relative of two of the four bases in DNA (adenine and guanine).
17. **Note: Splitting Water**: checkmark *(after: Mekanism Gases)*
   > What: the Electrolytic Separator (and MI's Electrolyzer) split water into hydrogen and oxygen.
   > Chemistry: 2H₂O → 2H₂ + O₂. Burning hydrogen releases energy; electrolysis pays that energy back to undo it.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > At the electrodes: the negative electrode (cathode) gives electrons to water: 2H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + 2OH⁻. The positive electrode (anode) takes them: 2H₂O → O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻.
   > Two to one: twice as much hydrogen gas as oxygen comes off, by volume, exactly as the formula says. MI matches it: 3000 mB water → 2000 mB H₂ + 1000 mB O₂.
   > Real detail: pure water barely conducts, so real cells add a dissolved salt or alkali (an electrolyte) to carry the current. Only the water is used up.
18. **Note: The Chlor-Alkali Process**: checkmark *(after: Chlorine)*
   > What: electrolysing salt gives chlorine, one of the most-used industrial chemicals (PVC, bleach, HCl).
   > Chemistry: in water: 2NaCl + 2H₂O → Cl₂ + H₂ + 2NaOH. Molten salt with no water gives metal: 2NaCl → 2Na + Cl₂.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Two routes: brine cells (the chlor-alkali process) make chlorine, hydrogen and sodium hydroxide (lye) together. Molten-salt cells (the Downs cell) make sodium metal and chlorine. Mekanism's separator gives sodium + chlorine, like the Downs cell.
   > Why chlorine goes to the anode: chloride ions (Cl⁻) carry the negative charge, so they're drawn to the positive electrode and give up their electrons: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂ + 2e⁻.
   > Next step: hydrogen and chlorine combine into hydrogen chloride, H₂ + Cl₂ → 2HCl, which you need for 4x ore and for PVC.
19. **Note: Catalysts**: checkmark *(after: Contact Process 2: SO₃)*
   > What: a catalyst speeds up a reaction without being consumed. Industry runs on them.
   > Chemistry: it offers an easier pathway with a lower energy barrier (activation energy). It doesn't change where the equilibrium ends up.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Classic catalysts: vanadium(V) oxide for SO₂ → SO₃ (contact process), iron for ammonia (Haber-Bosch), platinum-rhodium gauze for burning ammonia (Ostwald), chromium or titanium compounds for polyethylene, and zeolites in refinery cat-crackers.
   > Surface catalysts: gases stick to the metal surface, their bonds weaken, they react, and the product lets go, freeing the site for the next molecule.
   > In the pack: machines don't model catalysts, and MI's polymer recipes consume a few tiny metal dusts. In real plants the catalyst is replaced only when it slowly wears out or gets poisoned.
20. **Note: The Contact Process**: checkmark *(after: Contact Process 3: H₂SO₄)*
   > What: sulfuric acid is made in larger amounts than any other chemical: about 250 million tonnes a year.
   > Chemistry: S + O₂ → SO₂, then 2SO₂ + O₂ ⇌ 2SO₃ over V₂O₅, then SO₃ is turned into H₂SO₄.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > The balancing act: 2SO₂ + O₂ ⇌ 2SO₃ releases heat, so lower temperatures give more SO₃ but react slowly. About 450 °C with a catalyst is the compromise. This is Le Chatelier's principle in action.
   > The odd last step: SO₃ isn't bubbled straight into water; that makes a fine acid mist. It's absorbed into existing concentrated acid to form oleum (H₂S₂O₇), which is then diluted: H₂S₂O₇ + H₂O → 2H₂SO₄.
   > In the pack: Mekanism splits the steps across three machines; MI's Chemical Reactor does it all at once from sulfur, oxygen and water. Its uses: 5x ore, fertilisers, batteries and the Seed of a New World.
21. **Note: Haber-Bosch**: checkmark *(after: Haber-Bosch)*
   > What: ammonia, NH₃, made from nitrogen in the air and hydrogen. Fertiliser made from it feeds billions.
   > Chemistry: N₂ + 3H₂ ⇌ 2NH₃. The N≡N triple bond is one of the strongest bonds there is, so it needs pressure, heat and an iron catalyst.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Conditions: about 200 atmospheres and 400 to 450 °C over iron. The reaction releases heat, so cooler gives more ammonia but far too slowly; high pressure helps because 4 gas molecules become 2.
   > Recycling: only about 15% converts per pass, so the ammonia is condensed out and the unreacted gas goes round again.
   > Real hydrogen: most comes from natural gas (steam reforming, CH₄ + H₂O → CO + 3H₂). In the pack you get it from electrolysis instead, the 'green ammonia' route.
22. **Note: The Ostwald Process**: checkmark *(after: Ostwald Process)*
   > What: nitric acid, HNO₃, for fertiliser, explosives in mining, and in this pack, aqua regia.
   > Chemistry: overall NH₃ + 2O₂ → HNO₃ + H₂O, which happens in three real steps.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Step 1: ammonia burns over a red-hot platinum-rhodium gauze (about 900 °C): 4NH₃ + 5O₂ → 4NO + 6H₂O. The contact lasts only milliseconds.
   > Step 2: the gas cools and nitric oxide picks up more oxygen: 2NO + O₂ → 2NO₂ (brown).
   > Step 3: NO₂ is absorbed in water: 3NO₂ + H₂O → 2HNO₃ + NO. The leftover NO goes back to step 2.
   > In the pack: the Chemical Reactor does all three at once: 1 B ammonia + 2 B oxygen → 1 B nitric acid + 1 B water.
23. **Note: Fractional Distillation**: checkmark *(after: Fractional Distillation)*
   > What: crude oil is a mix of thousands of hydrocarbons. A distillation tower sorts them into fractions.
   > Chemistry: bigger molecules have stronger attractions between them, so they boil at higher temperatures.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > The tower: crude is heated to about 400 °C and fed in near the bottom. Vapour rises through trays, cooling as it goes; each fraction condenses at the height where the tower matches its boiling point.
   > Top to bottom: refinery gas (C₁ to C₄), naphtha (about C₅ to C₁₀), kerosene, diesel (light fuel), fuel oil (heavy fuel), and a tarry residue at the base.
   > Sour crude: crude contains sulfur compounds, which is why MI's first fractions are 'sulfuric'. Hydrotreating with hydrogen strips the sulfur out as H₂S, and in MI that comes back to you as sulfuric acid.
24. **Note: Steam Cracking**: checkmark *(after: Steam Cracking)*
   > What: cracking breaks big, less useful molecules into small, valuable ones, especially ethylene.
   > Chemistry: e.g. C₁₀H₂₂ → C₈H₁₈ + C₂H₄. One product must have a C=C double bond, because there isn't enough hydrogen to go round.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Steam cracking: naphtha mixed with steam is heated to about 850 °C for a fraction of a second, then quenched. The steam dilutes the hydrocarbon so it doesn't coke up the pipes. No catalyst: heat alone snaps the C-C bonds.
   > Catalytic cracking: refineries also crack heavy oil over zeolite catalysts at lower temperatures to make petrol. That's a different process with a different goal.
   > In the pack: naphtha + steam → steam-cracked naphtha in the Chemical Reactor, then the Distillation Tower separates ethylene, propene, benzene and more.
25. **Note: Polymers**: checkmark *(after: Polymerisation)*
   > What: a polymer is a giant molecule made of repeating small units (monomers). Polyethylene is the world's most common plastic.
   > Chemistry: n C₂H₄ → (-CH₂-CH₂-)ₙ. Each double bond opens and links to the next molecule: addition polymerisation.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Chain length: a typical polyethylene chain has tens of thousands of carbon atoms. Long chains tangle like spaghetti, which is why plastic is tough instead of waxy.
   > LDPE vs HDPE: high-pressure, radical polymerisation gives branched chains that pack loosely (soft LDPE film). Metal catalysts (Ziegler-Natta, Phillips) give straight chains that pack tightly (stiff HDPE, Mekanism's HDPE pellets).
   > In the pack: polyethylene unlocks HV digital circuits, and 16 buckets go into the Seed of a New World.
26. **Note: PVC**: checkmark *(after: PVC)*
   > What: polyvinyl chloride, the plastic of pipes, cable insulation and window frames. MI needs it for EV processing units.
   > Chemistry: n C₂H₃Cl → (-CH₂-CHCl-)ₙ. The same addition polymerisation as polyethylene, with one H swapped for Cl.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Why chlorine matters: the C-Cl bonds make PVC rigid, oil-resistant and slow to burn (it releases HCl, which smothers flames). That's why it's trusted around electrical wiring.
   > Making the monomer: the old route, used in MI, adds HCl to acetylene: C₂H₂ + HCl → C₂H₃Cl. Modern plants start from ethylene and chlorine instead, then crack the intermediate: C₂H₄Cl₂ → C₂H₃Cl + HCl.
   > Two industries meet: PVC is where petrochemicals (the carbon) and chlor-alkali (the chlorine) come together.
27. **Note: Silicon from Sand**: checkmark *(after: Silicon)*
   > What: silicon metal is made by stripping oxygen from quartz (SiO₂) with carbon.
   > Chemistry: SiO₂ + 2C → Si + 2CO at around 2000 °C in an electric arc furnace.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Metallurgical grade: this gives silicon about 98 to 99% pure. Good enough for aluminum alloys and silicones, nowhere near good enough for chips.
   > The purity ladder: for electronics, silicon is turned into a liquid (trichlorosilane: Si + 3HCl → SiHCl₃ + H₂), distilled until almost perfectly clean, then turned back into silicon (the Siemens process). That reaches 'nine nines', 99.9999999%.
   > In the pack: quartz dust + coke dust in the EBF gives silicon for transistors and diodes. The next purity step comes at EV.
28. **Note: Growing a Perfect Crystal**: checkmark *(after: Semiconductor Silicon)*
   > What: chips are built on wafers sliced from one single crystal of silicon.
   > Chemistry: a seed crystal dipped into molten silicon and pulled up slowly while turning grows into an ingot where every atom lines up.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Why argon: molten silicon reacts with oxygen and nitrogen, so the crystal is grown under argon, an unreactive noble gas. That's why the pack asks for argon here.
   > Doping: pure silicon conducts poorly. A few atoms of phosphorus per million (one extra electron each) make n-type silicon; boron (one electron short) makes p-type. Where the two meet you get a diode, and stacks of junctions make transistors.
   > In the pack: EBF: silicon dust + a tiny pinch of iridium + argon → monocrystalline silicon, then the Cutting Machine slices it into wafers.
29. **Note: Fission Power**: checkmark *(after: Fission)*
   > What: a fission reactor splits uranium nuclei and uses the heat to make steam.
   > Chemistry: ²³⁵U + n → ¹⁴¹Ba + ⁹²Kr + 3n + about 200 MeV. Mass numbers balance (236 = 141 + 92 + 3) and so do protons (92 = 56 + 36).
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Chain reaction: each split releases 2 to 3 neutrons. If on average exactly one causes another split, the reactor runs steadily. Control rods absorb extra neutrons to hold it there.
   > Where the energy comes from: the fragments weigh slightly less than the original nucleus. That missing mass becomes energy (E = mc²), about a million times more per atom than burning carbon.
   > Waste: the fragments are radioactive. In Mekanism, nuclear waste is processed into plutonium and polonium, which feed the next tiers. Always plan coolant and a SCRAM circuit.
30. **Note: Fusion Power**: checkmark *(after: Fusion)*
   > What: fusion joins hydrogen isotopes into helium. It powers every star.
   > Chemistry: ²H + ³H → ⁴He + n + 17.6 MeV. Per kilogram of fuel that's about four times more energy than fission.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > The hard part: nuclei are positively charged and repel each other. They must hit at enormous speed, which means a plasma at about 100 million °C, held away from the walls by magnetic fields or squeezed by lasers.
   > Ignition: laser fusion fires lasers into a tiny gold cylinder (a hohlraum) that turns the light into X-rays and crushes a fuel pellet. Mekanism borrows exactly that: a laser burst into a filled Hohlraum lights the reactor.
   > Fuel: deuterium comes from heavy water; tritium is bred from lithium: ⁶Li + n → ³H + ⁴He.
31. **Note: Antimatter as a Battery**: checkmark *(after: Antimatter)*
   > What: every particle has an antiparticle with the opposite charge. Meet and they annihilate into pure energy.
   > Chemistry: all the mass turns to energy: E = mc². One gram of antimatter meeting one gram of matter releases about 1.8 × 10¹⁴ J.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > Real antimatter: positrons (anti-electrons) are used every day in PET scans. Antiprotons and antihydrogen are made at CERN, but all of it ever made adds up to only nanograms, at an enormous energy cost.
   > A store, not a source: making antimatter always costs more energy than it gives back. That's why it works as a battery: put energy in once, carry it in a tiny pellet, get it out later.
   > In the pack: the SPS turns polonium and huge amounts of power into antimatter, which charges Antimatter Cores for the capstone. That part is game design, not real physics.
32. **Note: The Whole Notebook**: checkmark *(after: Seed of a New World)*
   > The last page of the notebook is blank. You realise you're meant to write it.
   > What: the Seed of a New World puts one product of every chemistry lesson into a single 9x9 recipe.
   > Chemistry: each ingredient stands for a whole industry you rebuilt from the ground up.
   > {@pagebreak}
   > The ledger: coke (pyrolysis and steelmaking), water and hydrogen (synthesis and electrolysis), sulfuric acid (the contact process), polyethylene (cracking and polymerisation), processing units (silicon purification and PVC), stainless plates (alloys), oganesson (building nuclei), plutonium and polonium (fission), Antimatter Cores (mass into energy).
   > The point: almost nothing in modern life is mined and used as-is. It's separated, reacted, purified and combined. That's chemistry, and now it's yours.
   > How: check JEI for the exact layout on the Ultimate Table.

### Mekanism

*Osmium, infusion and gases: Mekanism's machines, generators and its two big add-ons.*

1. **The Mekanism Way**: checkmark *(after: Mekanism: Metallurgic Infuser)*
   > Osmium hums in your hand. You remember metals that drank other substances and grew stronger.
   > What: Mekanism is a machine mod built on infusion and gases: soak a metal in carbon, redstone, diamond or refined obsidian and it becomes an alloy; pipe gases between machines and they become chemistry.
   > How: every machine comes in tiers (basic, advanced, elite, ultimate). In this pack each tier's control circuit needs the matching MI circuit.
   > Tip: this page is a side path. The main-path Mekanism quests are linked on the left; click one to jump there.
2. **Configurator**: `mekanism:configurator` *(after: The Mekanism Way)*
   > What: the Configurator sets which side of a Mekanism block takes input and gives output, ejects contents, and rotates blocks.
   > How: iron, an osmium ingot and steel. Sneak + right-click to change its mode; the side colours match the machine's GUI.
   > Tip: most "my machine won't take gas" problems are a side set to the wrong colour.
3. **Energy Tablet**: `mekanism:energy_tablet` *(after: The Mekanism Way, Infused Alloy)*
   > What: a portable FE store. It is also a crafting part in the Robit, the Wind Generator, lasers, Free Runners and the Portable Teleporter.
   > How: gold ingots, redstone and Infused Alloy (check JEI).
   > Tip: make a few at once; you'll keep needing them.
4. **Reinforced Alloy**: 4x `mekanism:alloy_reinforced` *(after: The Mekanism Way, Infused Alloy)*
   > What: the second Mekanism alloy, used in elite circuits, lasers and the Solar Neutron Activator.
   > Chemistry: real alloys work the same way: a few percent of a second element (carbon in steel, chromium in stainless) changes the metal's properties.
   > How: Metallurgic Infuser: Infused Alloy + diamond infusion (from diamonds or diamond dust; check JEI).
5. **Atomic Alloy**: 4x `mekanism:alloy_atomic` *(after: Reinforced Alloy)*
   > What: the top Mekanism alloy, needed for ultimate circuits, teleportation cores and the Robit.
   > How: first infuse obsidian dust with diamond to get refined obsidian dust, then infuse Reinforced Alloy with refined obsidian.
   > Tip: crush obsidian in the Crusher or an MI macerator for obsidian dust.
6. **Refined Obsidian**: `mekanism:osmium_compressor`; 4x `mekanism:ingot_refined_obsidian` *(after: Atomic Alloy, Advanced Control Circuit)*
   > What: refined obsidian ingots: very hard, blast-proof metal for teleporters, the Robit and endgame tools.
   > How: the Osmium Compressor presses refined obsidian dust with liquid osmium into ingots. It needs an Advanced Control Circuit (MV).
   > Tip: refined glowstone ingots come from the same machine: glowstone dust plus osmium.
7. **Advanced Control Circuit**: 2x `mekanism:advanced_control_circuit` *(after: Mekanism Circuits, MV: Aluminum)*
   > What: the circuit for every advanced-tier Mekanism block: factories, cubes, tanks, the Purification Chamber, and in this pack the whole 4x/5x ore line (injection, dissolution, washer, crystallizer).
   > How: an MI electronic circuit above two Infused Alloys and a Basic Control Circuit (check JEI).
   > Tip: the old infuser shortcut for circuits is removed in this pack. Circuits follow MI's voltage tiers.
8. **Elite Control Circuit**: 2x `mekanism:elite_control_circuit` *(after: Advanced Control Circuit, Reinforced Alloy, HV: Digital Circuits)*
   > What: the circuit for elite-tier blocks, the Combiner, the Security Desk and the Solar Neutron Activator.
   > How: an MI digital circuit above two Reinforced Alloys and an Advanced Control Circuit (check JEI).
   > Tip: HV comes with polyethylene on the main path. Make a stack of these before upgrading factories.
9. **Ultimate Control Circuit**: 2x `mekanism:ultimate_control_circuit` *(after: Elite Control Circuit, Atomic Alloy, EV: Processing Units)*
   > What: the circuit behind Mekanism's best blocks: the Isotopic Centrifuge, QIO, the Quantum Entangloporter, the Dimensional Stabilizer, the MekaSuit and the Meka-Tool.
   > How: an MI processing unit above two Atomic Alloys and an Elite Control Circuit (check JEI).
   > Tip: this unlocks the rest of the page: ultimate factories, QIO, More Machine's big machines and Mekanism Extras.
10. **Teleportation Core**: 2x `mekanism:teleportation_core` *(after: Refined Obsidian)*
   > What: the part inside teleporters, the Digital Miner, QIO drive arrays and the Quantum Entangloporter.
   > How: Atomic Alloy, gold ingots, a diamond and ender pearls (check JEI).
   > Tip: you'll need at least four of these for this page alone. Automate Atomic Alloy early.
11. **Teleporter**: `mekanism:teleporter`; 9x `mekanism:teleporter_frame` *(after: Teleportation Core)*
   > What: a frame-and-controller portal that links to other teleporters, even across dimensions.
   > How: the controller is a Teleportation Core in steel casings with basic circuits. Frames are refined obsidian around refined glowstone. Build a 3x4 frame with the controller in it (check JEI).
   > Tip: set both ends to the same frequency, and give the sending end plenty of power.
12. **Portable Teleporter**: `mekanism:portable_teleporter` *(after: Teleportation Core, Energy Tablet)*
   > What: a hand-held teleporter that jumps you to any teleporter frequency you own.
   > How: a Teleportation Core between two Energy Tablets and two basic circuits (check JEI). Charge it first.
   > Tip: the further the jump, the more energy it costs.
13. **Dimensional Stabilizer**: `mekanism:dimensional_stabilizer` *(after: Refined Obsidian, EV: Processing Units)*
   > What: Mekanism's chunk loader. It keeps chunks around it running while you're away, so your factories never pause.
   > How: refined obsidian, Atomic Alloy, Ultimate Control Circuits (EV) and a diamond block (check JEI). Pick the chunks in its GUI.
   > Tip: load only what you need. Every loaded chunk costs server time.
14. **Quantum Entangloporter**: 2x `mekanism:quantum_entangloporter` *(after: Teleportation Core, EV: Processing Units)*
   > What: two Entangloporters on the same frequency act as one block: energy, items, fluids, chemicals and heat go in one and out the other, across any distance or dimension.
   > Chemistry: real quantum entanglement can't carry information or matter faster than light (the no-communication theorem). Mekanism takes a little artistic licence.
   > How: refined obsidian, Atomic Alloy, Ultimate Control Circuits (EV) and a Teleportation Core (check JEI).
   > Tip: a pair is the easiest way to pipe uranium hexafluoride or D-T fuel from a remote plant to your base.
15. **Robit**: `mekanism:robit` *(after: Refined Obsidian)*
   > What: the Robit follows you, picks up items, smelts and crafts. It is also a crafting part for the Digital Miner and several More Machine blocks.
   > How: Atomic Alloy, refined obsidian, steel, an Energy Tablet and a personal chest (check JEI). Place it on a Chargepad to recharge.
   > Tip: rename it with a name tag. It won't mind.
16. **Digital Miner**: `mekanism:digital_miner` *(after: Robit, Teleportation Core)*
   > What: a machine that mines out a large radius below it, taking only the blocks you filter for.
   > How: Atomic Alloy, a Logistical Sorter, a Robit, a Teleportation Core and a steel casing (check JEI). Set filters (tags like c:ores work), radius and height, then start it.
   > Tip: it uses a lot of power. Speed upgrades help, but bring Energy upgrades too. Rich deposits sit at metal-specific depths: set its height range to match.
17. **QIO**: `mekanism:qio_drive_array`; `mekanism:qio_dashboard`; `mekanism:qio_drive_base` *(after: Ultimate Control Circuit)*
   > What: Quantum Item Orchestration: drives in a drive array hold items, and dashboards, importers and exporters reach them wirelessly through a frequency.
   > How: the drive array needs a Teleportation Core and Ultimate Control Circuits (EV); the base drive needs an ultimate circuit too. Give every QIO block the same frequency.
   > Tip: AE2 is the pack's main storage. QIO is a good wireless add-on for a remote outpost.
18. **QIO In and Out**: `mekanism:qio_importer`; `mekanism:qio_exporter`; `mekanism:portable_qio_dashboard` *(after: QIO, Polonium)*
   > What: importers pull items from the inventory they face into QIO; exporters push chosen items out. The Portable Dashboard opens your QIO from anywhere.
   > How: importer and exporter: lead, ender pearls, a Teleportation Core, an ultimate circuit and a (sticky) piston. The portable dashboard wraps a dashboard and a core in seven polonium pellets (check JEI).
   > Tip: an exporter with a filter on a machine's input is a tiny autocrafting line.
19. **Challenge: Denser Drives**: `mekanism:qio_drive_hyper_dense` *(after: QIO In and Out)*
   > What: the Hyper-Dense QIO Drive holds far more than a base drive. Time-Dilating and Supermassive drives go further still.
   > How: each tier wraps the one below with a Teleportation Core and rarer materials (check JEI for each).
   > Tip: drive count matters as much as size: an array holds 12 drives.
20. **Energized Smelter**: `mekanism:energized_smelter` *(after: Mekanism Circuits)*
   > What: Mekanism's electric furnace. It smelts anything a furnace can, takes upgrades, and is the base of the smelting factory.
   > Chemistry: electric heating is Joule heating: current through a resistance makes heat (P = I²R). No fuel, no smoke.
   > How: Basic Alloy, glass, a Basic Control Circuit and a steel casing (check JEI).
21. **Basic Factory**: 1x any of `or(item(mekanism:basic_smelting_factory)item(mekanism:basic_enriching_factory)item(mekanism:basic_crushing_factory)item(mekanism:basic_compressing_factory)item(mekanism:basic_combining_factory)item(mekanism:basic_purifying_factory)item(mekanism:basic_injecting_factory)item(mekanism:basic_infusing_factory)item(mekanism:basic_sawing_factory))` *(after: Mekanism Circuits, Energized Smelter)*
   > What: a factory is a machine with several slots that work in parallel. A basic factory runs 3 operations at once.
   > How: craft any single machine (Energized Smelter, Enrichment Chamber, Crusher...) with Basic Control Circuits and Basic Alloy. Any factory type counts.
   > Tip: factories sort their own inputs with auto-sort in the GUI, so one input pipe is enough.
22. **Advanced Factory**: 1x any of `or(item(mekanism:advanced_smelting_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_enriching_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_crushing_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_compressing_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_combining_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_purifying_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_injecting_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_infusing_factory)item(mekanism:advanced_sawing_factory))` *(after: Basic Factory, Advanced Control Circuit)*
   > What: 5 parallel slots, and faster with upgrades.
   > How: upgrade a basic factory in the crafting grid with Advanced Control Circuits, Infused Alloy and osmium, or use a Tier Installer on a placed one.
   > Tip: upgrading keeps the factory's type, so plan which machines you want at five slots.
23. **Elite Factory**: 1x any of `or(item(mekanism:elite_smelting_factory)item(mekanism:elite_enriching_factory)item(mekanism:elite_crushing_factory)item(mekanism:elite_compressing_factory)item(mekanism:elite_combining_factory)item(mekanism:elite_purifying_factory)item(mekanism:elite_injecting_factory)item(mekanism:elite_infusing_factory)item(mekanism:elite_sawing_factory))` *(after: Advanced Factory, Elite Control Circuit)*
   > What: 7 parallel slots. Pair an Elite Purifying or Injecting Factory with your ore line and the bottleneck moves elsewhere.
   > How: upgrade an advanced factory with Elite Control Circuits and Reinforced Alloy (check JEI).
   > Tip: the factory GUI shows each slot's progress; idle slots mean the input line is too slow.
24. **Ultimate Factory**: 1x any of `or(item(mekanism:ultimate_smelting_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_enriching_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_crushing_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_compressing_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_combining_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_purifying_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_injecting_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_infusing_factory)item(mekanism:ultimate_sawing_factory))` *(after: Elite Factory, Ultimate Control Circuit)*
   > Nine tasks in one box, all at once. You remember doing many things at once without effort.
   > What: 9 parallel slots, the top of base Mekanism. Mekanism Extras goes further (see the bottom band).
   > How: upgrade an elite factory with Ultimate Control Circuits and Atomic Alloy (check JEI).
   > Tip: add Speed and Energy upgrades: speed raises power use, energy upgrades bring it back down.
